Whoso considereth these words diligently, he findeth therein all that a godly Christian needeth
to know of this holy sacrament, as: First, what this holy sacrament is, namely the true body and
the true blood of our Lord Jesus Christ, which is given us under bread and wine to eat and to
drink, to sustain therewith not the body but the soul. Secondly, these words teach who hath
instituted and ordained this sacrament, namely no emperor, kings, man or angel, but our Lord
Jesus Christ himself, the emperor of all emperors, king of all kings, the Lord of the angels and
of all creatures, the redeemer of mankind, the only-begotten Son of God, who is with the Father
and the Holy Ghost of like power and truth, whose will and words come truly to pass and deceive
no man, though it seemed to our reason incomprehensible and impossible. Thirdly, these words
teach whereto the holy sacrament is profitable and useful to us, namely, first, that we thereby
are incorporated into our Lord Christ in faith, and he become one with us and we one with him,
unto eternal life, as he himself saith, John 6[:56 and 54]: Whoso eateth my flesh and drinketh
of my blood dwelleth in me and I in him, and I will raise him up again at the last day.
Secondly, it serveth us also thereto, that we thereby assure our faith in the promises of the
gospel of the forgiveness of sins, as with a pure, divine, undeceiving seal and pledge. Thirdly,
that we show forth the death of our Lord Christ until his coming again, that is, that we thank
him all the days of our life for the redemption, which hath come to pass not through gold or
silver (as Peter saith), but through his precious sacrifice and bloodshedding. Lastly, these
words teach who they are that receive the holy sacrament savingly to the forgiveness of their
sins, namely not the hypocrites, who appear penitent only before men and yet think not to amend
their sinful life, but remain after as before full of unchastity, hatred, envy, usury,
falsehood, deceit and the like; these eat and drink (as Paul saith) judgment to themselves. But
they that repent of their sins from the heart, and firmly believe the forgiveness thereof
through the passion, death and bloodshedding of Christ, and earnestly think to amend their life,
and so receive this holy sacrament hungry and thirsty for grace and mercy, as oft as they do it,
they are here assured by Christ that their sins are forgiven them and shall not be remembered.

That we may now receive the holy sacrament worthily, pray with me thus: Lord God, merciful
Father, we beseech thee, help us yet to a true repentance, and cover our sins and unworthiness
with the worthiness of thy dear Son Jesus Christ, our Lord, and grant that we may receive this
holy sacrament to the strengthening of our faith in the forgiveness of sins, and grow and
increase in a new life, that we, thus clothed with the worthiness of thy Son, may be eternally
saved, through the same thy Son, our Lord Christ. Amen.

**What follows.** "Eyn ander" (another): the editor notes that the order then gives the
exhortation "Myn alderlevesten in Godt", which Lüneburg 1564 also has in High German, that is,
the Braunschweig recension of Osiander (§5.4), and that Buxtehude adds the wish "Datt vorlene
uns Godt alle, dat wy idt werdichlick entfangen. Amen" (God grant this to us all, that we
receive it worthily. Amen; Sehling 7/1, p. 125). Buxtehude is thus also a witness of §5.4.

#### 5.14.4 Micron, London 1554, German 1565

Micron's order for the Dutch stranger church at London (Reformed), in the German version of 1565
(with the Norden Dutch variants), has the minister read the institution from 1 Cor 11 from the
pulpit and then exhort the congregation to examine itself "ihe sie an den tisch des Herren
sitze" (before it sit at the table of the Lord). **Micron, *Ordinancien* (1554), German 1565**
(Reformed; Sehling 7/1, pp. 631–633):

<!-- doc 2116 -->
> Ihr habt, lieben brüder, auß dieser lehre des heiligen apostels Pauli gehöret, wer der
> einsetzer des nachtmals sey, nemlich Christus Jesus, und wie es sol gehalten werden, es sey
> denn, das man in grosse gefahr der seelen fallen wil. Denn sie werden schuldig an dem leib und
> blut Christi, essen und trinken ihnen selbs das gericht, die es unwirdig empfahen. Auf daß wir
> denn diser gefahr entfliehen und das nachtmal des Herren wirdiglich niessen mögen, so lasset
> uns, ihe wir an den tisch des Herren sitzen, fleissig anhören, warin (nach der heiligen
> schrift) die rechte niessung des nachtmals gelegen sey. Dieselbige stellet hie der apostel
> Paulus für in unserer prüffung und in dem underscheiden des leibs des Herren, also daß ein
> jeder, der sich selbs […] aufrichtig prüffet und den leib des Heeren underscheidet, wirdiglich
> zu seiner seelen trost und seligkeit zum nachtmal des Herren geht. Diese prüffung aber und
> underscheidung des leibs des Herren oder seines opfers, durch welches er seinen leib für uns
> in den tod gegeben hat, ist in dreien stücken fürnemlich gelegen. Zum ersten, daß wir unsere
> sünden, die wir wider Gott und unsern nechsten getan haben, von herzen bekennen und beweinen
> und uns (sofern es müglich) warhaftig mit allen denen versünen, mit welchen wir etwas
> widerwertiges haben. Zum andern, das wir in der erkentnuß unser sünden und in der empfindnuß
> des zorns Gottes gegen uns nicht kleinmütig werden umb unser sünden willen noch in
> verzweifelung fallen an seiner güte gegen uns, auch unsere zuflucht nicht nemen zu
> menschlichen verdiensten, sonder sollen festiglich glauben, daß uns alle unsere sünden allein
> auß lauter gnaden umbsonst vergeben werden durch das einige verdienst des tods Christi, gleich
> ob wir den todt selber in unser eignen person am stammen des kreuzes gelidten hetten. Zum
> dritten ist das rechte prüffen unsers gewissens hierin gelegen, das wir einfeltig one
> gleißnerey vor uns nemen ein aufrichtige besserung des lebens nach dem heiligen willen Gottes.
> Hierin soll denn ein jeder sein eigen herz und gewissen fleissig undersuchen, wie er sich
> selbs gerüst find gegen Gott und seinen nechsten; denn welcher halßstarrig in seinen sünden
> wil bleiben, als in abgötterey, lästerung, geiz, stelen, liegen, betriegen, haderen,
> volsaufen, unreinigkeit und dergleichen, oder der seine sünden bekennet und doch in jemand
> anders beruwet denn in Jesu Christo und in dem verdienst seines tods, oder der nicht von
> ganzem herzen one gleißnerey vor sich nimpt, ein besser leben zu füren, derselbige sol nu, zu
> dem nachtmal des Herren zu kommen, nicht eilen. So aber jemand anders hierin handlen wurde,
> wie heimlich auch dasselbige geschehe, wird er doch vor dem angesicht Gottes schuldig sein des
> tods Christi, welchen er also verunehrt und verachtet.
>
> Diß sagen wir dennoch darumb nicht, lieben brüder, als wolten wir die zerschlagene herzen der
> glaubigen kleinmütig machen, als wen niemand zu dem nachtmal des Herren möcht gehn, denn der
> von aller unreinigkeit der sünden frey und in aller heiligkeit volkommen sey. Denn Christus
> der Herr hat sein nachtmal nicht eingesetzt zu einem zeugnuß unser heiligkeit oder
> volkommenheit, sonder vielmehr zu einem zeugnuß unser unvolkommenheit und ungerechtigkeit,
> welche er von uns hinnimpt durch das opfer seines leibs und rechent uns seine gerechtigkeit
> und ehre zu, ja, schenket uns dieselbige. Und darumb kommen die gläubigen zu dem tisch des
> Herren nit, daß sie sich ihrer gerechtigkeit und fromkeit rümen, sonder vilmehr zu bezeugen,
> daß sie auß ihnen selbs nichts anders sünd [!] denn kinder des zorns, under die sind [!]
> verkauft und unwirdig, ihre augen gehn himmel aufzuheben, welcher gerechtigkeit, volkommenheit
> und heiligkeit allein Christus ist, der alle unsere krankheit auf sich durch sein kreuz
> genomen hat und geust uns ein durch den heiligen Geist ein rechte lust zu der gerechtigkeit.
>
> Derhalben, so vil er under uns sind, die zum trost ihres gewissens mit dem hochzeitlichen
> kleide an den tisch des Herren sitzen wollen: Lasset uns unß selbs von ganzem herzen der
> sünden halben beschüldigen und vestiglich glauben, daß sie uns allein umb Christus willen
> nicht zugerechnet werden. Lasset uns auch für uns nemmen ein ernstliche besserung des lebens.
> Und auf daß wir die süssigkeit des verdienstes des tods Christi in dem gebrauch des nachtmals
> in unsern herzen kreftiglich empfinden und geniessen mögen, so sollen wir nit kleben bleiben
> in der eusserlichen ubung oder in den elementen des brots und weins, sonder dardurch vermanet,
> müssen wir unsere sinne, herzen und verstand hinauf in himmel erheben, da Jesus Christus
> allein nach dem leib ist, ein getreuer fürsprecher für uns, und dardurch sollen wir
> ungezweifelt versichert sein, daß wir viel gewisser durch die geistliche gemeinschaft des
> leibs und bluts Christi zu dem ewigen leben gespeiset werden, denn unsere sterbliche leibe
> durch speise und trank teglich underhalten werden, und denn wir in dem nachtmal des Herren
> brot und wein nach dem befelch Christi niessen. Gott, unser himlischer Vater, wolle uns seine
> gnade geben, daß wir die warhaftige speise unser seelen Jesum Christum in unserm gewissen
> warhaftig zu dem ewigen leben schmecken mögen. Amen.

Ye have heard, dear brethren, out of this doctrine of the holy apostle Paul, who is the
institutor of the Supper, namely Christ Jesus, and how it shall be kept, unless one will fall
into great danger of the soul. For they that receive it unworthily become guilty of the body and
blood of Christ, and eat and drink judgment to themselves. That we then may flee this danger and
enjoy the Supper of the Lord worthily, let us, before we sit at the table of the Lord, hear
diligently wherein (according to the holy scripture) the right eating of the Supper lieth. The
same doth the apostle Paul here set forth in our proving and in the discerning of the body of
the Lord, so that every one that sincerely proveth himself and discerneth the body of the Lord
goeth worthily to the Supper of the Lord to his soul's comfort and salvation. But this proving
and discerning of the body of the Lord, or of his sacrifice, whereby he hath given his body into
death for us, lieth chiefly in three points. First, that we confess and bewail from the heart
our sins, which we have done against God and our neighbour, and truly reconcile ourselves (so
far as it be possible) with all those with whom we have aught at variance. Secondly, that in the
knowledge of our sins and in the feeling of the wrath of God against us we become not
faint-hearted for our sins' sake, nor fall into despair of his goodness toward us, nor yet take
our refuge in human merits, but shall firmly believe that all our sins are forgiven us only out
of pure grace, freely, through the only merit of the death of Christ, even as if we ourselves
had suffered the death in our own person on the tree of the cross. Thirdly, the right proving of
our conscience lieth herein, that we simply, without hypocrisy, purpose a sincere amendment of
life according to the holy will of God. Herein then shall every one diligently search his own
heart and conscience, how he findeth himself prepared toward God and his neighbour; for whoso
will remain stiff-necked in his sins, as in idolatry, blasphemy, covetousness, stealing, lying,
deceiving, quarrelling, drunkenness, uncleanness and the like, or who confesseth his sins and
yet trusteth in any other than in Jesus Christ and in the merit of his death, or who purposeth
not with his whole heart, without hypocrisy, to lead a better life, the same shall not now make
haste to come to the Supper of the Lord. But if any should deal otherwise herein, however
secretly the same be done, he shall yet before the face of God be guilty of the death of Christ,
whom he thus dishonoureth and despiseth.

Yet we say not this, dear brethren, as though we would make the broken hearts of the faithful
faint, as if none might go to the Supper of the Lord but he that is free from all uncleanness of
sins and perfect in all holiness. For Christ the Lord hath not instituted his Supper for a
witness of our holiness or perfection, but much rather for a witness of our imperfection and
unrighteousness, which he taketh from us by the sacrifice of his body, and reckoneth to us his
righteousness and honour, yea, giveth us the same. And therefore the faithful come to the table
of the Lord, not that they may boast of their righteousness and godliness, but much rather to
testify that of themselves they are nothing else than children of wrath, sold under sin, and
unworthy to lift up their eyes to heaven, whose righteousness, perfection and holiness is Christ
alone, who hath taken all our sickness upon himself through his cross, and poureth into us
through the Holy Ghost a right desire for righteousness.

Therefore, as many of us as will sit at the table of the Lord for the comfort of their
conscience with the wedding garment: Let us accuse ourselves with our whole heart for our sins,
and firmly believe that they are not imputed to us, only for Christ's sake. Let us also purpose
an earnest amendment of life. And that we may mightily feel and enjoy in our hearts the
sweetness of the merit of the death of Christ in the use of the Supper, we shall not remain
cleaving to the outward exercise or to the elements of bread and wine, but, admonished thereby,
we must lift up our senses, hearts and understanding into heaven, where Jesus Christ alone is
according to the body, a faithful advocate for us; and thereby we shall be assured without doubt
that we are fed unto eternal life through the spiritual communion of the body and blood of
Christ much more surely than our mortal bodies are daily sustained by meat and drink, and than
we eat bread and wine in the Supper of the Lord according to the commandment of Christ. God, our
heavenly Father, give us his grace, that we may truly taste the true food of our souls, Jesus
Christ, in our conscience unto eternal life. Amen.

**Notes.**
- **The place.** It is read after the Verba, from the pulpit; the minister then goes down to the
  table and announces 1 Cor 5:7f.
  ("Nach diser vermanung gehet der diener von der kanzel und stellet sich zu den andern dinern bey dem tisch", after this exhortation the minister goeth from the pulpit and standeth with the other ministers by the table; Sehling 7/1, p. 633).
- **Sources.** Sehling's editor compares the second point, on the forgiveness won "am galgen des
  krützes" (on the gallows of the cross), with the third exhortation of Gellius Faber of Emden
  (Reformed) (Sehling 7/1, p. 632).
- **The *Sursum corda* turned to a doctrine.** "Wir müssen unsere sinne, herzen und verstand
  hinauf in himmel erheben, da Jesus Christus allein nach dem leib ist" (we must lift up our
  senses, hearts and understanding into heaven, where Jesus Christ alone is according to the
  body) is the Reformed reading of *Sursum corda*.
- **Moers** (Reformed). The Lower Rhine recension of the Palatinate exhortation (§5.10) opens
  with Micron's words: "Auß dieser lehr deß heiligen apostels Pauli hört ihr …" (Out of this
  doctrine of the holy apostle Paul ye hear …).

#### 5.14.5 Three short notes

- **Marienhafe, *Kirchenordnung*, 1593.** After communion "tut der prediger ein kurze vermanung
  von der liebe des nehesten" (the preacher maketh a short exhortation of the love of the
  neighbour), on 1 Cor 10:17 and the figure of many grains and grapes made into one bread and
  one wine. It turns into the thanksgiving: "so hebet nun eure herzen zu Godt (ihr geliebten)
  und last uns frolick danksagen"
  (so lift up now your hearts to God, beloved, and let us joyfully give thanks; Sehling 7/1, p. 713).
  The image of grains and grapes is the one that closes the Palatinate exhortation of 1563
  (Reformed; §5.10). Sehling's editor also describes the Low German Nürnberg hospital Mass, "De
  düdesche Misse" of a Hamburg hymnal of 1558, which has both the Nürnberg and the Bugenhagen
  exhortations after the *Agnus Dei* (p. 714).
- **Hohenlohe, *Kirchenordnung*, 1578.** Besides the Brandenburg-Nürnberg exhortation at the
  altar (§5.5), the order appends "Vermanungen vom heiligen abendmal, an dem sambstag zur vesper
  den beichtkindern und communicanten vorzulesen"
  (exhortations of the holy Supper, to be read at Saturday Vespers to the penitents and communicants; Sehling 15, p. 327).
  They are a cycle of eight teaching homilies with a common opening, "Lieben freunde Christi.
  Dieweil wir morgendes tags das hochwirdige und heilige abendmal … zu halten gedenken" (Dear
  friends of Christ, forasmuch as we purpose to keep tomorrow the most worthy and holy Supper),
  which begins with the words of Luther's address (§5.3). The editor traces them to the Saturday
  sermons of Johann Hofmann (pp. 243, 328).
- **Mecklenburg 1540/45.** Its three Low German exhortations are not printed by Sehling (§5.2).

---

## 6. Before and after the service

### 6.1 Vesting prayers

**No vesting prayers survive.** None of the evangelical orders in Sehling prints or appoints a
vesting prayer, or any of the other prayers in the Lutheran Missal Project's *Vesting Prayers*:
- the psalms of preparation (84, 85, 86, 116, 130) with the antiphon *Ne reminiscaris*;
- the Kyrie, Our Father and *preces*;
- the collects;
- the prayers before vesting, at the washing of the hands, and at the amice, alb, cincture,
  maniple, stole, tunicle, chasuble and mitre.

The searches covered the Latin incipits (*Ne reminiscaris*, *Quam dilecta*, *Benedixisti*,
*Inclina, Domine*, *Credidi*, *De profundis* in a preparatory setting), *praeparatio ad missam*,
*amictus*, *indue me*, and the German and Low German words for vesting. The psalms occur only in
the Office and the Introits.

The only mention of the medieval prayers is an editor's note. Sehling's editor, on the Amberg
hospital order of 1544, distinguishes the three preparations of the priest before Mass.
**Amberg, *Ordnung der kirchen* … *im spital*, 1544, editor's note** (Sehling 13, p. 282):

<!-- doc 410 -->
> Was hier in diesem beachtenswerten Versuch als Rüsthandlung für die Gemeinde anstatt des
> Stufengebetes (mit dem Confiteor) als einer Rüsthandlung des zelebrierenden Geistlichen vor
> den Introitus gesetzt ist, ist wahrscheinlich eine Form des Akzesses. Dieser ist die erste der
> drei vorgeschriebenen Rüsthandlungen des Geistlichen. Diese war nicht - wie die auf ihn
> folgende Praeparatio ad missam (u.a. mit den Gebeten bei Anlegung der einzelnen Bestandteile
> der Meßkleidung) und die dann schon in der Messe selbst in Gestalt des Stufengebetes - im
> Meßbuch ihrer Form nach genau vorgeschrieben, konnte daher örtlich und persönlich durchaus
> verschieden sein und auch die Form einer Andacht, mit der Priester sich gemeinsam auf die
> Feier der Messe vorbereiteten, annehmen

What is here set before the Introit, in this noteworthy attempt, as a preparation for the
congregation instead of the prayers at the foot of the altar (with the Confiteor), which are a
preparation of the celebrating clergyman, is probably a form of the *accessus*. This is the
first of the three prescribed preparations of the clergyman. It was not, like the *Praeparatio
ad missam* that followed it (with, among other things, the prayers at the putting on of the
several parts of the Mass vesture), and that which then came within the Mass itself in the form
of the prayers at the foot of the altar, exactly prescribed in its form in the Missal, and could
therefore differ quite widely by place and person, and could also take the form of a devotion
with which priests prepared themselves together for the celebration of Mass.

**What the orders do say about vesting.** The orders mention vesting only as a rubric, and only
to say that it signifies nothing. The vestments themselves are indifferent. **Brandenburg,
*Kirchenordnung*, 1540** (Sehling 3, p. 65):

<!-- doc 1746 -->
> Die andern zusetz eusserlicher leiblicher ding, als messgewand, altardeck, silbern und gülden
> gefess, lichter etc. sein aller ding frei, geben und nemen dem glauben und gewissen nichts.
> Darum dieweil sie vorhin verhanden und gezeuget sein, sol man sie behalten und brauchen,
> sonderlich die kleider darum, das die diener der kirchen nicht alweg in iren eigen kleidern
> also gestalt sein, das sie darinnen tapfer und ehrlich der gemein mochten dienen.

The other additions of outward bodily things, as Mass vestment, altar cloth, silver and golden
vessels, lights, etc., are altogether free; they give and take nothing to faith and conscience.
Therefore, since they were there before and have been provided, they shall be kept and used,
especially the vestments, because the ministers of the church are not always so furnished in
their own clothes that they might serve the congregation therein worthily and honourably.

The rubrics on vesting say no more than this:
- **The moment of vesting.** At Hadeln the priest vests during the *Te Deum* at the end of
  Matins, "nicht alse nödig, sundern als ein düchtig erbarlich zirath, und nicht darinne de
  saligkeit soken" (not as needful, but as a seemly and honest ornament, and not seeking
  salvation therein; §3.4). In Mecklenburg 1540 the priest turns to the people "wenn he sick
  hefft angekledet" (when he hath vested himself; §3.4). In both, vesting is done in silence, in
  the church or sacristy, with no prayer.
- **The *Stufengebet***, the third of the medieval preparations, is the only one that survives
  (§3.2–3.4). The *accessus* survives, recast, only as the congregation's *Veni Sancte Spiritus*
  (§3.7).

### 6.2 The psalms and the *Ne reminiscaris*

None of the five preparatory psalms of the Missal's *praeparatio* is appointed as a private
preparation of the minister, and the antiphon *Ne reminiscaris* (Tobit 3:3) does not occur.
- **The nearest thing** is Bugenhagen's *Domine, ne memineris iniquitatum nostrarum antiquarum*
  (Ps 79:8), said with the sexton at the altar step (§3.3). It is the same plea for the
  forgetting of old sins, but taken from the Lenten tract.
- **Penitential psalms.** Pfalz-Neuburg 1543 allows "ein feinen bußpsalm" (a goodly penitential
  psalm) in place of the *Confiteor* (§3.2). Ps 130 *De profundis* appears as a hymn, Luther's
  *Aus tiefer Not*, sung as the Introit in Lippe (§3.4) and often elsewhere.
- **The psalms themselves** occur in the corpus only as Introits, Office psalms, or proof texts.
  Ps 84 *Quam dilecta* is the dedication psalm at Hof 1592 (Sehling 11, p. 422), and Lüneburg
  1564 cites it in defence of public worship (Sehling 6/1, p. 541).

### 6.3 After the Mass

**The priest's private thanksgiving.** The medieval *gratiarum actio post missam* (the canticle
*Benedicite*, Ps 150 *Laudate Dominum in sanctis eius*, and collects, said by the priest while
unvesting) survives in only one form. In Bugenhagen's Hildesheim order of 1544 and the
Schleswig-Holstein order of 1542, the priest unvests during the closing hymn, kneels again
before the altar, and "danket Godde hemelick vor sick sülvest" (thanketh God secretly for
himself; Sehling 7/2.1, p. 855; Sehling 23, p. 91). No text is given. Both are quoted with
translations in [`CANON_IN_GERMAN_MASSES.md`](CANON_IN_GERMAN_MASSES.md) §§15.2–15.3.

**The Benedicite and Ps 150.** Neither is appointed after Mass in any evangelical order. Both
occur only as the canticle and psalm of **Sunday Lauds**, sung in the choir by the schoolboys or
the canons. Pomerania prescribes the weekday canticles of Lauds after the *Te Deum*.
**Pomerania, *Pia ordinatio caeremoniarum*, 1535** (Sehling 4, p. 349):

<!-- doc 1857 -->
> Post tertium responsorium, quod cum gloria patri etc. canitur, sequatur te deum etc. Mox
> cantor incipiat aliquam antiphonam, secundum cuius tonum canatur tantum unus ex psalmis
> prophetalibus, quae cantica vocantur. — Dominica die: Benedicite omnia etc. -— Feria secunda:
> Confitebor tibi, domine, quoniam iratus etc. — Feria tertia: Ego dixi etc. —- Feria quarta:
> Exultavit etc. — Feria quinta: Cantemus etc. — Feria sexta: Domine, audivi etc. — Sabbato:
> Audite coeli etc.

After the third responsory, which is sung with the Gloria Patri, etc., let the Te Deum follow,
etc. Next let the cantor begin some antiphon, according to whose tone there shall be sung only
one of the prophetic psalms, which are called canticles: on the Lord's Day, *Benedicite omnia*,
etc.; on Monday, *Confitebor tibi, Domine, quoniam iratus*, etc.; on Tuesday, *Ego dixi*, etc.;
on Wednesday, *Exultavit*, etc.; on Thursday, *Cantemus*, etc.; on Friday, *Domine, audivi*,
etc.; on Saturday, *Audite coeli*, etc.

The Schleswig-Holstein order of 1542 has the same scheme in Low German, "des Sondages:
Benedicite Omnia etc." (on Sundays: *Benedicite omnia*; Sehling 23, p. 141). At the Ansbach
collegiate churches Sunday Lauds keeps both the *Benedicite* and the *Laudate* psalms.
**Brandenburg-Ansbach, *Ordnung singens und lesens bei den Stiften*, 1533**
(Sehling 11, p. 313):

<!-- doc 276 -->
> Am sontag singen sie das Te Deum laudamus. Darnach die psalmen Dominus regnavit, Jubilate Deo
> omnis terra, Deus Deus meus, Benedicite omnia opera, Laudate Dominum de coelis mit der
> antiphona Indutus est, Benedictus Dominus etc., wie man sunst gewonlich am sontag helt.

On Sunday they sing the Te Deum laudamus; thereafter the psalms *Dominus regnavit*, *Jubilate
Deo omnis terra*, *Deus, Deus meus*, *Benedicite omnia opera*, *Laudate Dominum de coelis*, with
the antiphon *Indutus est*; the *Benedictus Dominus*, etc., as is otherwise customarily held on
Sunday.

**Who sings them.** At Ansbach, "sie": the canons and vicars of the collegiate foundations. In
Pomerania and Schleswig-Holstein, the cantor and the schoolboys in the choir. The people are not
present at Lauds. The *Laudate* psalms (Pss 148–150) are sung under the one heading "Laudate
Dominum de coelis".

**What the orders do appoint after Mass.** In place of the priest's private thanksgiving, the
evangelical Mass ends with a public thanksgiving by all:
- a postcommunion collect, usually Luther's "Wir danken dir, allmächtiger Herr Gott";
- the Aaronic blessing;
- a hymn or psalm sung by the schoolboys and people while the communicants return, or at the
  very end. At Hildesheim it is "einen düdeschen, korten psalm" (a short German psalm) begun by
  the schoolmaster.

In Kantz 1522 and in Nürnberg the *Nunc dimittis* is sung after communion. The collects and
thanksgiving prayers are treated in [`COLLECTS_GUIDE.md`](COLLECTS_GUIDE.md), and the hymns in
[`HYMN_PRACTICE_GUIDE.md`](HYMN_PRACTICE_GUIDE.md).

### 6.4 Prayers before and after the sermon

**The pattern.** Every Lutheran order surrounds the sermon with prayer. The preacher calls the
people to prayer before he begins, and they pray, usually the Our Father in silence or aloud, or
sing a hymn to the Holy Ghost. At the end of the sermon he calls them again to thanksgiving and
to intercession for the Church, the government and all need, and leads the general prayer. The
Lüneburg order puts it most fully. **Lüneburg, *Kirchenordnung*, 1564**
(Sehling 6/1, pp. 544–545):

<!-- doc 2003 -->
> Aber vor der predigt hat der priester das volk zum christlichen gebet zu vermanen, das der
> liebe Gott ihnen seinen heiligen Geist geben wolle, sein heiliges wort nützbarlich zu handeln
> […] und in das herze zu fassen, das sie ihr sündlich leben bessern, trost und sterkung ihres
> glaubens erlangen und hinfurt christlich und seliglich leben mögen. Darauf sol man ein Vater
> unser sprechen oder singen: Nu bitten wir den heiligen Geist. Umb die Weihnachten: Ein
> kindelein so löbelich etc. Umb die Ostern: Christ ist erstanden etc. Zum ende der predigt sol
> das volk abermal zum gebet und danksagung gegen Gott vermanet werden und samptlich vor
> erhaltung der kirchen Gottes und rechter lere, darzu der treuen warhaftigen lerer und das Gott
> treue arbeiter in seine ernte senden wolle, für die oberkeit, für zeitlichen friede und
> gewechs der früchte und in summa für die not der ganzen christenheit und sonderbarer personen,
> die des christlichen gebets begeren

But before the sermon the priest is to exhort the people to Christian prayer, that the dear God
would give them his Holy Ghost, to handle his holy word profitably and to take it into the
heart, that they may amend their sinful life, obtain comfort and strengthening of their faith,
and henceforth live christianly and blessedly. Thereupon an Our Father shall be said, or *Nu
bitten wir den heiligen Geist* sung; about Christmas, *Ein kindelein so löbelich*, etc.; about
Easter, *Christ ist erstanden*, etc. At the end of the sermon the people shall again be exhorted
to prayer and thanksgiving toward God, and [shall pray] together for the preservation of the
Church of God and right doctrine, and thereto of faithful, true teachers, and that God would
send faithful labourers into his harvest; for the government, for temporal peace and the
increase of the fruits; and in sum for the need of all Christendom and of particular persons
that desire the Christian prayer.

**Other orders.**
- **Luther 1526**: "das man auf der cantzel gemein gebet thut oder das vater unser fur spricht"
  (that the common prayer is made in the pulpit, or the Our Father said before [the people]),
  remains from the old pulpit office (§5.3).
- **Mecklenburg 1552**: at the end of the sermon the people are "zum gebet und danksagung …
  vermanet" (exhorted to prayer and thanksgiving), and the preacher is to bid them "das sie in
  der kirchen bei der communio bleiben" (that they remain in the church at the communion)
  (Sehling 5, p. 198). Lüneburg copies it.
- **Hadeln**: the preacher "schall thom ersten dat volk vormahnen tho vlitiger anropinge der
  hulpe gades" (shall first exhort the people to diligent calling upon the help of God). After
  the sermon he again exhorts to prayer, recites the Small Catechism, and prays for the prince,
  the preachers "und alle nothsaken" (and all needs) (Sehling 5, p. 468).
- **Buxtehude 1552**: the chaplains "scholen … alle ore predige mit gesange, Godt umme syne
  gnade to bidden, ahnfangen"
  (shall begin all their sermons with a hymn, to pray God for his grace; Sehling 7/1, p. 72).
- **Hanau-Lichtenberg 1573**, weekday sermons: "fur der Predig eynen Psalmen singen. Dem folg
  ein Vater unser und die Predig. Letzlich beschließ die Predig mit dem Gesang, Gebet und Segen"
  (before the sermon sing a psalm; then follow an Our Father and the sermon; lastly close the sermon with hymn, prayer and blessing; Sehling 20/2, p. 53).
  In the villages without a school the pastor sings a German psalm, then "erzele er die offne
  Beicht, Absolution und Epistel" (let him recite the open confession, absolution and epistle;
  ibid.).
- **Palatinate 1563** (Reformed): the prayer of confession before the sermon, ending in the Our
  Father, said by the minister (§3.6); the confession, absolution and the long general prayer
  after it (§4.4).

The pulpit prayers, the greeting and the notices are treated more fully in
[`SERMONS_GUIDE.md`](SERMONS_GUIDE.md) §10.

**Who prays.** The preacher bids and leads; the congregation prays the Our Father with him or
silently, and answers "Amen". The schoolboys and choir sing the hymn. No order gives a private
prayer for the preacher to say before going up into the pulpit. Pfalz-Veldenz 1574 has him begin
the sermon "mitt dem gebett, wie breuchlich" (with the prayer, as is customary; Sehling 18, p.
499), and leaves the text to custom.

### 6.5 On entering and leaving church; the communicants' own prayers

**On entering church.** No order in the corpus prints or appoints a prayer for the worshipper to
say on entering or leaving the church. The modern hymnal's "prayer on entering church" has no
sixteenth-century church-order counterpart. What the orders appoint "when one comes into church"
is a hymn:
- **Sayn 1590**: "Erstlich, wann man in die Kirche kompt, soll man einen Teutschen Psalm singen.
  Darnach soll der Pfarrherr ein Collect lesen" (First, when one cometh into the church, a
  German psalm shall be sung; thereafter the pastor shall read a collect; Sehling 19/1, p. 412).
- **Pfalz-Zweibrücken 1557**: an opening hymn of the Holy Ghost is sung "biß die Kirch zusammen
  kombt" (until the church cometh together; §3.4).
- **Amberg 1550**, weekday morning prayer: the priest begins *Deus in adiutorium*, and the choir
  sings one or two Latin psalms "bis das volk zusammenkumbt" (until the people come together;
  Sehling 13, p. 287).

The orders regulate behaviour instead: the people are not to stand about in the churchyard or
run in and out during the sermon. The village ordinance of Obereisenheim (Castell) of 1579 wants
each to go into church when the bell is rung, to "Gottes wort hören, der predigt auswarten und
sein gebet mit einer ganzen gemeind ruhig und still volbringen" (hear God's word, wait upon the
sermon, and finish his prayer with the whole congregation quietly and still; Sehling 11, p.
688).

**The communicants' prayers.** The orders print no private prayers for the communicant to say
before or after receiving. What the communicant does is set out in three ways:
1. **Private preparation** by confession and examination on the Saturday (§4.6).
2. **Prayers said for them by the minister**:
   - Pfalz-Veldenz 1574 has a "Gebett vor dem Abendmal", read by the minister to the people
     after the absolution. It asks "du wöllest uns allen, die wir von disem deinem heiligen
     abendtmal essen unnd trinckhen werden, die seelige gemeinschafft deines leibs und bluts
     gnediglich schenckhen"
     (that thou wouldest graciously give unto all of us that shall eat and drink of this thy holy Supper the blessed communion of thy body and blood; Sehling 18, p. 499).
   - Micron's Dutch order (Reformed) has a "Gebett vor dem nachtmal" (prayer before the Supper),
     said by the minister from the pulpit while all kneel (Sehling 7/1, p. 630).
   - Ysenburg 1588 prints more than one prayer after communion, all collects of the minister
     (Sehling 10, p. 627).
3. **Hymns sung during the distribution.** *Jesus Christus unser Heiland*, *Gott sei gelobet*,
   the German *Agnus Dei*: the hymns are the people's prayer during communion. Mecklenburg 1552
   tells the communicants "ir solt auch hiebei euer herzlich gebet thun" (ye shall also here
   make your hearty prayer), and gives the prayer in the first person, to be made their own
   (§5.9).

**The hymnal.** The nearest thing to the modern hymnal's printed devotions is the
Pfalz-Zweibrücken *Gesangbuch* of 1571/1587. It reprints the Mecklenburg confession, prayer and
absolution as "Beicht, Gebett und Absolutio vor der Predig am Sontag" (Confession, prayer and
absolution before the sermon on Sunday), so that the people had the text in their hands
(Sehling 18, p. 187, apparatus).

**A note on "Benedicite".** In the household sections of the orders, "Benedicite" and "Gratias"
are the grace before and after meat of Luther's Small Catechism ("Aller augen warten auff dich,
Herr" and "Dancket dem Herrn, dann er ist freundlich"). It is not the canticle of the three
children. Pfalz-Zweibrücken 1557 heads it "Wie ein Haußvater sein Gesinde soll leren, das
Benedicite und Gratias sprechen" (How a householder shall teach his household to say the
Benedicite and Gratias; Sehling 18, p. 180).

---

## 7. Table by order

The table gives, for each order discussed in §3 and §4 and for the orders with an exhortation of
their own (§5.14), what stands before the Introit, how the confession is said and absolved, and
where the exhortation falls. Every cell has been checked against the text of the order. "—"
means the order has nothing of the kind; "not stated" means the order mentions the item but
gives no text or form. "A"–"G" are the families of §3.1; absolution types 1–5 are those of §4.4.
The orders that appear only as witnesses to an exhortation are listed in the witness tables of
§5.3–5.13.

| Order | Opening before the Introit | Confession: who and where | Absolution (type) | Exhortation (family; place) | § |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Kantz, Nördlingen 1522 | Exhortation, absolution, *Adiutorium*, *Veni Sancte Spiritus* (D) | No confession text; before the Sacrament | "sag euch frei, ledig und los" (3) | Free, with a short address leading to the absolution; before the Preface | 3.5, 4.3, 5.14.1 |
| Müntzer, Allstedt 1523 and 1524 (Radical Reformation) | Ps 43 with priest and people; priest's confession; people's *Misereatur*; *Deus, tu conversus*; *Non nobis*; *Aufer a nobis* (G) | Priest, aloud "vor allem volk"; the people answer | — | — | 3.8 |
| Volprecht, Nürnberg 1524 | *Offene beicht* "anstatt des Confiteor" (D) | Priest, to God and the brothers and sisters | "Gott hat sich unser erbarmet" (2) | Osiander (V); printed after the Mass, for days with communicants | 3.5, 5.4 |
| Wertheim 1524 (Kolb) | Sermon | Priest kneeling, "Confiteor brevissimum" in German, after the sermon | — | None before communion ("sine omni adhortatione"); a short exhortation to thanksgiving after communion, and one "pro praeparatione" in the sermon | 3.6 |
| Döber, Nürnberg 1525 | Address, *Adiutorium*, *Confitemini*, confession, *Misereatur*, declaration, *Veni Sancte Spiritus* (C) | Priest kneeling; people "sprecht mir nach" | Optative (1), then declaration (2) | Osiander; after the Our Father | 3.4, 5.4 |
| Bremen, *Evangelische Misse*, 1525 | Address, absolution, *Adiutorium*, *Veni Sancte Spiritus* (D) | No confession text; the priest asks the people's prayer ("Bicht des presters") | "vorkundige iw fry, ledich unde los" (3) | Free, with Kantz's address in Low German; before the Preface | 3.5, 5.14.1 |
| Luther, *Deutsche Messe* 1526 | German hymn or psalm (F) | — | — | Luther; after the sermon, pulpit or altar | 3.7, 5.3 |
| Hadeln ("1526") | Priest vests during the *Te Deum*; *Veni Sancte Spiritus* kneeling (C, F) | Priest sings it kneeling; the sexton answers | Read by the sexton; later given up | Bugenhagen ("Mine allerlevesten etc."); after the sermon, before the Preface | 3.4, 5.7 |
